I just never seem to be able to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: During this time when you've been running on this motor, how much sleep have you been getting, and do you feel like you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I need less sleep at all. I still try to get my usual seven or eight hours. I mean, sometimes it's hard to actually wind down and fall asleep because my brain is still just... going, but I definitely still need the rest. I'm usually pretty exhausted by the time I get home from teaching, so yeah, it's not like I'm staying up all night feeling energized or anything. I definitely still need a normal amount of sleep.\n\nDoctor: When did this feeling of constantly running on a motor start? Was there a specific time when it began, or has it always been like this for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't always been quite this intense. I mean, this specific level of trouble has been going on for about eight months now. That was right around the time our older relative moved in with us. So, yeah, it's been about eight months of just feeling like I can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Besides this feeling of constantly being in motion, have you been experiencing excessive worry about things, or feeling tense and on edge? And has your mood been irritable or short-tempered during these eight months?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've been excessively worried or tense. I mean, I have the normal stresses of life and work, but I'm not sitting around worrying about things all the time. And as for my mood... no, I haven't been irritable or short-tempered. I just feel like I can't stop moving, but it hasn't really made me angry or on edge with anyone.\n\nDoctor: When your older relative moved in eight months ago, did you notice this feeling of constantly running on a motor start right around that time, or did it begin a bit later after they had already moved in?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure on the exact timing. I just know that the past eight months or so have been really, really hard. It feels like around the time they moved in is when I really started noticing that I just couldn't turn the motor off, but I don't know if it was the exact day or a few weeks later. It's just all kind of blurred together into this constant feeling of needing to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health around the time this started—things like headaches, dizziness, changes in appetite, or any other physical symptoms that feel different from your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, physically, I feel pretty much the same as always. I haven't had any headaches or dizziness, and my appetite is normal. It's really just that constant urge to be moving, but as far as my actual physical health or feeling sick... no, nothing like that. Everything else has stayed at my usual baseline.\n\nDoctor: When your relative moved in eight months ago, did your life circumstances change in ways that felt stressful or difficult—like changes in your daily routine, your privacy, or your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, sure, having someone move in changes the house dynamics a little bit, but... it wasn't some huge stressor. My routine pretty much stayed the exact same. I still went to school, taught my classes, and came home. I didn't really feel like I lost my privacy or suddenly had all these new responsibilities piled on me. So, no, I wouldn't say the circumstances themselves were difficult or stressful like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical evaluations or blood work done? I'm asking because sometimes thyroid problems or other medical conditions can cause this kind of constant restless feeling you're describing.\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I haven't had a physical or any blood work done recently. I mean, I usually only go if I'm feeling sick, and since I've felt fine physically, I just haven't been in to see my regular doctor. So, no recent tests or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes around the time this started—such as changes in your weight, energy level despite getting sleep, or how you feel temperature-wise? Or have you had any recent infections, illnesses, or been started on any new medications?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. My weight has been pretty steady, and my energy is... well, it's normal, I guess. I mean, I'm restless, but I'm not fatigued or anything. Temperature-wise, I feel completely fine. And I haven't been sick, no infections, and I'm not taking any new medications. Honestly, physically, everything is just... normal.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling this constant urge to move and do things, are your thoughts racing as well? Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're thinking about because your mind is jumping from one thing to another?\n\nPatient: No, my thoughts don't really do that. It's not like my mind is jumping all over the place or racing. I can keep track of what I'm thinking about just fine. It's really more of a... a physical thing. Like my body just has to keep moving and doing something, even though my head is perfectly clear.\n\nDoctor: Besides this restless feeling and the constant urge to move, have you noticed any other changes in how you think, feel, or behave over these past eight months—anything unusual that feels different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's just the restlessness. I don't feel sad or anxious, and my mood is pretty much the same as it's always been. I haven't started acting out of character or doing anything weird. It's just... I can't sit still, and I always feel like I need to be up and doing something. Other than that, everything else feels completely normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And has your mood been irritable or short-tempered during these eight months?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've been excessively worried or tense. I mean, I have the normal stresses of life and work, but I'm not sitting around worrying about things all the time. And as for my mood... no, I haven't been irritable or short-tempered. I just feel like I can't stop moving, but it hasn't really made me angry or on edge with anyone.\n\nDoctor: When your older relative moved in eight months ago, did you notice this feeling of constantly running on a motor start right around that time, or did it begin a bit later after they had already moved in?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure on the exact timing. I just know that the past eight months or so have been really, really hard. It feels like around the time they moved in is when I really started noticing that I just couldn't turn the motor off, but I don't know if it was the exact day or a few weeks later. It's just all kind of blurred together into this constant feeling of needing to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health around the time this started—things like headaches, dizziness, changes in appetite, or any other physical symptoms that feel different from your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, physically, I feel pretty much the same as always. I haven't had any headaches or dizziness, and my appetite is normal. It's really just that constant urge to be moving, but as far as my actual physical health or feeling sick... no, nothing like that. Everything else has stayed at my usual baseline.\n\nDoctor: When your relative moved in eight months ago, did your life circumstances change in ways that felt stressful or difficult—like changes in your daily routine, your privacy, or your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, sure, having someone move in changes the house dynamics a little bit, but... it wasn't some huge stressor. My routine pretty much stayed the exact same. I still went to school, taught my classes, and came home. I didn't really feel like I lost my privacy or suddenly had all these new responsibilities piled on me. So, no, I wouldn't say the circumstances themselves were difficult or stressful like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical evaluations or blood work done? I'm asking because sometimes thyroid problems or other medical conditions can cause this kind of constant restless feeling you're describing.\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I haven't had a physical or any blood work done recently. I mean, I usually only go if I'm feeling sick, and since I've felt fine physically, I just haven't been in to see my regular doctor. So, no recent tests or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes around the time this started—such as changes in your weight, energy level despite getting sleep, or how you feel temperature-wise? Or have you had any recent infections, illnesses, or been started on any new medications?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. My weight has been pretty steady, and my energy is... well, it's normal, I guess. I mean, I'm restless, but I'm not fatigued or anything. Temperature-wise, I feel completely fine. And I haven't been sick, no infections, and I'm not taking any new medications. Honestly, physically, everything is just... normal.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling this constant urge to move and do things, are your thoughts racing as well? Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're thinking about because your mind is jumping from one thing to another?\n\nPatient: No, my thoughts don't really do that. It's not like my mind is jumping all over the place or racing. I can keep track of what I'm thinking about just fine. It's really more of a... a physical thing. Like my body just has to keep moving and doing something, even though my head is perfectly clear.\n\nDoctor: Besides this restless feeling and the constant urge to move, have you noticed any other changes in how you think, feel, or behave over these past eight months—anything unusual that feels different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just... I can't sit still, and I always feel like I need to be up and doing something. Other than that, everything else feels completely normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating or focusing on tasks, or any problems with your memory—like forgetting things you normally wouldn't forget—during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My memory is fine, I haven't been forgetting things or anything. And focusing... no, I can concentrate just fine on what I'm doing. I teach special ed, so I have to stay pretty sharp for my students, and I haven't had any issues doing that. Like I said, my head is clear, it really is just that physical need to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any new substances in the past eight months—such as caffeine, energy drinks, stimulant medications, or anything else—that might have increased around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't started taking any new medications, and... no, no energy drinks or anything. I drink coffee, but just my usual amount. I haven't increased it or changed anything about my routine since all this started. It's just the same as it's always been.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, have there been any other significant changes or stressful events in your life—anything at all, whether at work, in your relationships, or anywhere else—that you've been dealing with or thinking about?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, there hasn't been anything like that. I mean, work is always a little demanding—teaching special ed has its moments, of course—but it's nothing new or different. No big changes at home or in my relationships, either. Everything has been pretty steady, just my normal routine. There really haven't been any major stressors or life events lately that would explain why I'm suddenly feeling so wound up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's just all kind of blurred together into this constant feeling of needing to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health around the time this started—things like headaches, dizziness, changes in appetite, or any other physical symptoms that feel different from your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, physically, I feel pretty much the same as always. I haven't had any headaches or dizziness, and my appetite is normal. It's really just that constant urge to be moving, but as far as my actual physical health or feeling sick... no, nothing like that. Everything else has stayed at my usual baseline.\n\nDoctor: When your relative moved in eight months ago, did your life circumstances change in ways that felt stressful or difficult—like changes in your daily routine, your privacy, or your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, sure, having someone move in changes the house dynamics a little bit, but... it wasn't some huge stressor. My routine pretty much stayed the exact same. I still went to school, taught my classes, and came home. I didn't really feel like I lost my privacy or suddenly had all these new responsibilities piled on me. So, no, I wouldn't say the circumstances themselves were difficult or stressful like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical evaluations or blood work done? I'm asking because sometimes thyroid problems or other medical conditions can cause this kind of constant restless feeling you're describing.\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I haven't had a physical or any blood work done recently. I mean, I usually only go if I'm feeling sick, and since I've felt fine physically, I just haven't been in to see my regular doctor. So, no recent tests or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes around the time this started—such as changes in your weight, energy level despite getting sleep, or how you feel temperature-wise? Or have you had any recent infections, illnesses, or been started on any new medications?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. My weight has been pretty steady, and my energy is... well, it's normal, I guess. I mean, I'm restless, but I'm not fatigued or anything. Temperature-wise, I feel completely fine. And I haven't been sick, no infections, and I'm not taking any new medications. Honestly, physically, everything is just... normal.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling this constant urge to move and do things, are your thoughts racing as well? Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're thinking about because your mind is jumping from one thing to another?\n\nPatient: No, my thoughts don't really do that. It's not like my mind is jumping all over the place or racing. I can keep track of what I'm thinking about just fine. It's really more of a... a physical thing. Like my body just has to keep moving and doing something, even though my head is perfectly clear.\n\nDoctor: Besides this restless feeling and the constant urge to move, have you noticed any other changes in how you think, feel, or behave over these past eight months—anything unusual that feels different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's just the restlessness. I don't feel sad or anxious, and my mood is pretty much the same as it's always been. I haven't started acting out of character or doing anything weird. It's just... I can't sit still, and I always feel like I need to be up and doing something. I teach special ed, so I have to stay pretty sharp for my students, and I haven't had any issues doing that. Like I said, my head is clear, it really is just that physical need to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any new substances in the past eight months—such as caffeine, energy drinks, stimulant medications, or anything else—that might have increased around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't started taking any new medications, and... no, no energy drinks or anything. I drink coffee, but just my usual amount. I haven't increased it or changed anything about my routine since all this started. It's just the same as it's always been.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, have there been any other significant changes or stressful events in your life—anything at all, whether at work, in your relationships, or anywhere else—that you've been dealing with or thinking about?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, there hasn't been anything like that. It feels like around the time they moved in is when I really started noticing that I just couldn't turn the motor off, but I don't know if it was the exact day or a few weeks later. It's just all kind of blurred together into this constant feeling of needing to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health around the time this started—things like headaches, dizziness, changes in appetite, or any other physical symptoms that feel different from your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, physically, I feel pretty much the same as always. I haven't had any headaches or dizziness, and my appetite is normal. It's really just that constant urge to be moving, but as far as my actual physical health or feeling sick... no, nothing like that. Everything else has stayed at my usual baseline.\n\nDoctor: When your relative moved in eight months ago, did your life circumstances change in ways that felt stressful or difficult—like changes in your daily routine, your privacy, or your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, sure, having someone move in changes the house dynamics a little bit, but... it wasn't some huge stressor. My routine pretty much stayed the exact same. I still went to school, taught my classes, and came home. I didn't really feel like I lost my privacy or suddenly had all these new responsibilities piled on me. So, no, I wouldn't say the circumstances themselves were difficult or stressful like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical evaluations or blood work done? I'm asking because sometimes thyroid problems or other medical conditions can cause this kind of constant restless feeling you're describing.\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I haven't had a physical or any blood work done recently. I mean, I usually only go if I'm feeling sick, and since I've felt fine physically, I just haven't been in to see my regular doctor. So, no recent tests or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes around the time this started—such as changes in your weight, energy level despite getting sleep, or how you feel temperature-wise? Or have you had any recent infections, illnesses, or been started on any new medications?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. My weight has been pretty steady, and my energy is... well, it's normal, I guess. I mean, I'm restless, but I'm not fatigued or anything. Temperature-wise, I feel completely fine. And I haven't been sick, no infections, and I'm not taking any new medications. It's not like my mind is jumping all over the place or racing. I can keep track of what I'm thinking about just fine. It's really more of a... a physical thing. Like my body just has to keep moving and doing something, even though my head is perfectly clear.\n\nDoctor: Besides this restless feeling and the constant urge to move, have you noticed any other changes in how you think, feel, or behave over these past eight months—anything unusual that feels different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just... I can't sit still, and I always feel like I need to be up and doing something. Was there a specific time when it began, or has it always been like this for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't always been quite this intense. I mean, this specific level of trouble has been going on for about eight months now. That was right around the time our older relative moved in with us. So, yeah, it's been about eight months of just feeling like I can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Besides this feeling of constantly being in motion, have you been experiencing excessive worry about things, or feeling tense and on edge? And has your mood been irritable or short-tempered during these eight months?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've been excessively worried or tense. I mean, I have the normal stresses of life and work, but I'm not sitting around worrying about things all the time. And as for my mood... no, I haven't been irritable or short-tempered. I just feel like I can't stop moving, but it hasn't really made me angry or on edge with anyone.\n\nDoctor: When your older relative moved in eight months ago, did you notice this feeling of constantly running on a motor start right around that time, or did it begin a bit later after they had already moved in?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure on the exact timing. I just know that the past eight months or so have been really, really hard. It feels like around the time they moved in is when I really started noticing that I just couldn't turn the motor off, but I don't know if it was the exact day or a few weeks later. It's just all kind of blurred together into this constant feeling of needing to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health around the time this started—things like headaches, dizziness, changes in appetite, or any other physical symptoms that feel different from your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, physically, I feel pretty much the same as always. I haven't had any headaches or dizziness, and my appetite is normal. It's really just that constant urge to be moving, but as far as my actual physical health or feeling sick... no, nothing like that. Everything else has stayed at my usual baseline.\n\nDoctor: When your relative moved in eight months ago, did your life circumstances change in ways that felt stressful or difficult—like changes in your daily routine, your privacy, or your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, sure, having someone move in changes the house dynamics a little bit, but... it wasn't some huge stressor. My routine pretty much stayed the exact same. I still went to school, taught my classes, and came home. I didn't really feel like I lost my privacy or suddenly had all these new responsibilities piled on me. So, no, I wouldn't say the circumstances themselves were difficult or stressful like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical evaluations or blood work done? I'm asking because sometimes thyroid problems or other medical conditions can cause this kind of constant restless feeling you're describing.\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. It's just... I can't sit still, and I always feel like I need to be up and doing something.
